You will be assessed on this consistency.\n    14→\n    15→---\n    16→\n    17→## Project Context\n    18→\n    19→- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n    20→- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference papers and code repository. \n    21→- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n    22→- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n    23→\n    24→## Reference Information\n    25→\n    26→- The reference **papers**:\n    27→  - \"A brain-wide map of neural activity during complex behaviour\" describes how the data were collected and is in the file `datapaper.pdf`.\n    28→  - \"Exploiting correlations across trials and behavioral sessions to improve neural decoding\" describes how the code were used and is in the file `methodpaper.pdf`.\n    29→  - A white paper about the data architecture is in `dataarchitecture.pdf\n    30→- Some parts of the papers that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n    31→- **Code** from the methods paper are in the directory `code`.\n    32→- **Data** from the data paper are in the directory `data`. Some data may be missing and may need to be downloaded. \n    33→- **Documentation** about how to access the data can be found at https://docs.internationalbrainlab.org/public_docs/public_introduction.html.\n    34→\n    35→## Decoder Task\n    36→\n    37→Temporally align based on stimulus onset\n    38→\n    39→### Decoder Inputs:\n    40→- Time since stimulus onset, continuous, time-varying\n    41→- Trial number in block, continuous, per-trial\n    42→\n    43→### Decoder Outputs\n    44→- Choice, binary, per-trial, left = 0, right = 1\n    45→- Prior probability of left, per-trial, 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2\n    46→- Wheel speed discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\n    47→- Whisker motion energy discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\n    48→\n    49→## Target Data Format\n    50→\n    51→- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n    52→- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n    53→\n    54→```python\n    55→data = {\n    56→    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n    57→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    58→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n    59→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n    60→            ...\n    61→        ],\n    62→        [  # list of trials for session 2\n    63→            ...\n    64→        ],\n    65→        ...\n    66→    ],\n    67→\n    68→    'input': [  # list of sessions\n    69→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    70→            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n    71→            ...\n    72→        ],\n    73→        ...\n    74→    ],\n    75→\n    76→    'output': [  # list of sessions\n    77→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    78→            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n    79→            ...\n    80→        ],\n    81→        ...\n    82→    ],\n    83→\n    84→    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    85→    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n    86→\n    87→    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    88→    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n    89→        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    90→    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n    91→\n    92→    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    93→    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    94→    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n    95→        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n    96→        ...\n    97→    ],\n    98→\n    99→    'metadata': {\n   100→        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n   101→        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n   102→        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n   103→        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n   104→        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n   105→        ...\n   106→    }\n   107→}\n   108→```\n   109→\n   110→### Key Format Requirements\n   111→\n   112→- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n   113→  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n   114→  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n   115→  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n   116→\n   117→- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n   118→  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   119→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   120→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   121→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   122→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   123→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   124→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   125→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   126→\n   127→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   128→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   129→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   130→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   131→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   132→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   133→\n   134→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   135→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   136→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   137→\n   138→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   139→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   140→\n   141→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   142→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   143→\n   144→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   145→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   146→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   147→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   148→\n   149→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   150→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   151→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   152→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   153→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   154→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   155→\n   156→## Python environment\n   157→\n   158→- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n   159→- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n   160→- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n   161→\n   162→## Decoder Reference\n   163→\n   164→- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   165→- Optional arguments:\n   166→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   167→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   168→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   169→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   170→\n   171→### Purpose\n   172→\n   173→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   174→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   175→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   176→3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   177→4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n   178→5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   179→\n   180→### Consistency requirements\n   181→\n   182→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n   183→- Loading of data\n   184→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   185→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   186→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   187→\n   188→To check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n   189→\n   190→Whenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n   191→\n   192→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n   193→\n   194→Carefully verify your work after every step. Each step has \"Done when\" criteria — verify them before moving on.\n\n3. **MATCH THE REFERENCE PROCESSING**: Your processing must match what's described in the reference papers and code. You will be assessed on this consistency.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference papers and code repository. \n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **papers**:\n  - \"A brain-wide map of neural activity during complex behaviour\" describes how the data were collected and is in the file `datapaper.pdf`.\n  - \"Exploiting correlations across trials and behavioral sessions to improve neural decoding\" describes how the code were used and is in the file `methodpaper.pdf`.\n  - A white paper about the data architecture is in `dataarchitecture.pdf\n- Some parts of the papers that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n- **Code** from the methods paper are in the directory `code`.\n- **Data** from the data paper are in the directory `data`. Some data may be missing and may need to be downloaded. \n- **Documentation** about how to access the data can be found at https://docs.internationalbrainlab.org/public_docs/public_introduction.html.\n\n## Decoder Task\n\nTemporally align based on stimulus onset\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time since stimulus onset, continuous, time-varying\n- Trial number in block, continuous, per-trial\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Choice, binary, per-trial, left = 0, right = 1\n- Prior probability of left, per-trial, 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2\n- Wheel speed discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\n- Whisker motion energy discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \n\nYou will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference paper and code and these instructions. During testing, the reference version of `train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \n\n---\n\n## Conversion Workflow\n\n**IMPORTANT**: Follow these steps in order. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n- Write efficient code:\n  - Vectorize loops\n  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n  - There are no temporal misalignments\n  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n- Write clear, well-documented functions\n- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n\n**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n\n**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n\n---\n\n### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n\n**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n\n**Actions**:\n1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\n  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n  b. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\n4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \n6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n\n**Done when**:\n- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n\n**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n\n---\n\n### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n\n**Check 1: Output log verification**\nRead through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n\n**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n\n**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n- Fix any mismatches. \n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n\n**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference papers. \n- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n- Fix any discovered issues. \n- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n\n**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n\nWrite a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n\n**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n\n---\n\n### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n\n**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n\n**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n\n**Actions**:\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \n\n**Done when**: \n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n\n---\n\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers's accuracy for each variable\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \n\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n\n**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n\n---\n\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n\n**Actions**:\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n  - Key decisions and rationale\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n  - Validation results and tables\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\n  - Dataset description\n  - How to load and use the converted data\n  - Output format specification\n  - Key statistics\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\n- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n\n---\n\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n     4→\n     5→i. Everything is read through the ONE api against the IBL cache in `data/one_cache`, never by opening files directly. The index of the brain-wide map release is loaded first, listing the sessions and the datasets each one holds. Searching that index returns an `eid`, the session identifier, and that one string is the only handle needed afterwards: every loader takes the `eid` and resolves the files itself. `SessionLoader` reads the trials table, the wheel and the camera motion energy of a session, and `SpikeSortingLoader` reads the spikes and clusters of one probe insertion, so it is called once per probe.\n     6→\n     7→ii. The API resolves a session and a dataset name to a path. Use the one.search to idenity unique session id to then locate the data files.\n    20→\n    21→## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n    22→\n    23→i. The search returns the subject name alongside each `eid`, so no parsing of paths or file names is involved. Sessions are collected into a dictionary keyed by subject, which is the form the conversion iterates over. At assembly the subjects are the sorted unique names and `subject_idx` records, for each session, its index into that list. The 444 converted sessions come from 136 subjects, a median of three sessions per animal and up to thirteen.\n    24→\n    25→ii. Grouping the sessions as they are selected:\n    26→```python\n    27→selected.setdefault(session['subject'], []).append(str(eid))\n    28→```\n    29→\n    30→At assembly:\n    31→```python\n    32→subjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\n    33→sub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n    34→```\n    35→\n    36→iii. The API already returns a unique subject id, so nothing has to be derived.\n    37→\n    38→## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n    39→\n    40→i. A session is already the unit the release is organised by, and the search returns one `eid` per session, so nothing has to be split.\n    41→\n    42→ii. The search returns the session identifiers:\n    43→```python\n    44→eids, info = one.search(query_type='local', details=True)\n    45→```\n    46→\n    47→iii. No decision to make, the API returns sessions individually.\n    48→\n    49→## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n    50→\n    51→i. The trials table has one row per trial, so the split is given by the data.\n    52→\n    53→ii. The trials table of a session:\n    54→```python\n    55→loader.load_trials()\n    56→```\n    57→\n    58→iii. No decision to make, the trials table is already one row per trial.\n    59→\n    60→## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n    61→\n    62→i. Four things, all applied together. The reaction time, first wheel movement minus stimulus onset, has to fall between 80 ms and 2 s. Trials where the animal made no choice are dropped. Trials whose choice or block probability is missing are dropped. Finally the trial window has to be spanned by the wheel and the camera, which drops trials at the edges of a recording.\n    63→\n    64→ii.\n    65→```python\n    66→keep = (reaction >= MIN_RT) & (reaction <= MAX_RT)          # NaN compares false, so those go too\n    67→keep &= trials.choice.isin(CHOICE).to_numpy()               # drops the no response trials\n    68→keep &= trials.probabilityLeft.isin(PRIOR).to_numpy()\n    69→\n    70→return keep & covered(wheel_times, stim_on) & covered(camera_times, stim_on)\n    71→```\n    72→\n    73→iii. The reaction time bounds and the no-response exclusion are the reference paper's own trial mask, `min_rt=0.08, max_rt=2` with `exclude_nochoice=True`, and the data paper truncates its reaction time distribution at the same 80 ms and 2 s.\n    74→\n    75→## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n    76→\n    77→i. Two arrays per probe, `spikes.times` and `spikes.clusters`, which are the time of every spike and the cluster it was assigned to. The cluster table supplies the quality label and the anatomical location, but the neural array itself is built only from those two.\n    78→\n    79→ii. Both come from the spike sorting loader:\n    80→```python\n    81→spikes, clusters, channels = loader.load_spike_sorting(check_hash=False)\n    82→```\n    83→\n    84→```python\n    85→units.append(new_index[spikes['clusters'][keep]] + len(regions))\n    86→times.append(spikes['times'][keep])\n    87→```\n    88→\n    89→iii. Spike time is directly avialible through the loader.\n    90→\n    91→## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n    92→\n    93→i. Spikes are counted into 20 ms bins over the trial window, giving one count per unit per bin, and the counts are divided by the bin width to become a firing rate in Hz. When a session has two probes their units are pooled into one population, numbered continuously so that the second probe's units continue after the first probe's.\n    94→\n    95→ii. Counting a trial, then converting to a rate:\n    96→```python\n    97→bin_index = np.floor((spike_time - T_START) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n    98→flat_index = spike_unit * N_BINS + bin_index\n    99→counts[trial] = np.bincount(flat_index, minlength=n_units * N_BINS).reshape(n_units, N_BINS)\n   100→```\n   101→\n   102→```python\n   103→rate = spike_counts(spike_times, spike_units, regions.size, stim_on) / BIN\n   104→```\n   105→\n   106→iii. The reference bins spike counts and does not smooth, so no smoothing is applied here either. Merging the probes of a session follows the reference's `merge_probes`, whose reasoning is that two probes in one session see the same behaviour and so are not independent recordings.\n   107→\n   108→## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n   109→\n   110→i. Only clusters whose `label` is 1 are kept. The label is a score of 0, 1/3, 2/3 or 1 that the IBL pipeline assigns from a set of spike sorting metrics, so requiring 1 keeps the units that pass all of them. Across the release this keeps 75,708 of 621,733 clusters, and in the converted data 73,044 units over 444 sessions, a median of 142 per session.\n   111→\n   112→ii. The filter, applied per probe before the spikes are kept:\n   113→```python\n   114→# spikes.clusters holds the row of the cluster table, so a per cluster flag\n   115→# can be indexed by it directly to say whether each spike survives\n   116→is_good = clusters['label'] >= QC_LABEL\n   117→keep = is_good[spikes['clusters']]\n   118→```\n   119→\n   120→iii. This is the curation of the data paper, which reports 75,708 well-isolated neurons averaging 108 per probe; applying `label >= 1` reproduce their number.\n   121→\n   122→## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n   123→\n   124→i. Every stream of a session is already expressed in seconds on one shared clock: spike times, trial event times, wheel timestamps and camera frame times all run from the start of the recording, because IBL synchronises them upstream through a sync channel common to the ephys and the behaviour rig. Aligning a trial is therefore just subtracting its stimulus onset from the spike times, which puts zero at the event and leaves the window running from -0.5 s to 1.5 s around it.\n   125→\n   126→ii. Subtracting the onset:\n   127→```python\n   128→spike_time = times[begin[trial]:end[trial]] - onset      # time from stimulus onset\n   129→```\n   130→\n   131→iii. Because the clocks are already aligned there is additional alignment required.\n   132→\n   133→## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n   134→\n   135→i. The 2 s window is divided into 100 bins of 20 ms; no resample or interpolation is applied.\n   136→\n   137→ii. The bin grid, and the bin each spike falls in:\n   138→```python\n   139→BIN = 0.02\n   140→N_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))            # 100\n   141→```\n   142→\n   143→```python\n   144→bin_index = np.floor((spike_time - T_START) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n   145→```\n   146→\n   147→iii. The reference code sets `'binsize': 0.02`, and both papers use 20 ms bins; the method paper describes exactly this configuration, \"split into 2-s trials, each divided into 20-ms bins, producing T = 100 time steps\".\n   148→\n   149→## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *time_from_stimulus_onset* derived from?\n   150→\n   151→i. From `stimOn_times` in the trials table, which is the event every trial is aligned on. The window is -0.5 to 1.5 s around that onset in 20 ms bins, and the input is the centre of each of the 100 bins, so one set of values serves every trial.\n   152→\n   153→ii. The onset each trial is aligned on, and the window taken around it:\n   154→```python\n   155→stim_on = trials.stimOn_times.to_numpy()[keep]\n   156→```\n   157→```python\n   158→T_START, T_STOP = -0.5, 1.5      # decoding window around stimulus onset, following the reference code\n   159→BIN = 0.02\n   160→N_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))            # 100\n   161→EDGES = T_START + BIN * np.arange(N_BINS + 1)\n   162→TIME = EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                              # bin centres, the first decoder input\n   163→```\n   164→\n   165→iii. The window and the bin size are the decoding parameters of the reference code.\n   166→\n   167→## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *time_from_stimulus_onset*?\n   168→\n   169→i. There is no processing, the variable is defined by us.\n   170→\n   171→ii. The centres, and the input of one trial built from them:\n   172→```python\n   173→T_START, T_STOP = -0.5, 1.5      # decoding window around stimulus onset, following the reference code\n   174→BIN = 0.02\n   175→N_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))            # 100\n   176→EDGES = T_START + BIN * np.arange(N_BINS + 1)\n   177→TIME = EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                              # bin centres, the first decoder input\n   178→```\n   179→\n   180→iii. N/A\n   181→\n   182→## 3-c. How is `input` *time_from_stimulus_onset* aligned with the neural data?\n   183→\n   184→i. It is the neural binning grid itself: the spikes are binned on `EDGES` around each trial's `stimOn_times`, and the input is the centre of those same bins, so bin for bin the two describe the same instant.\n   185→\n   186→ii. The grid the spikes are binned on, and the centres of it:\n   187→```python\n   188→bin_index = np.floor((spike_time - T_START) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n   189→```\n   190→```python\n   191→TIME = EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2\n   192→```\n   193→\n   194→iii. N/A\n   195→\n   196→## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *trial_number_in_block* derived from?\n   197→\n   198→i. From `probabilityLeft` in the trials table, which is held constant within a block, so a change of its value starts a new one.\n   199→\n   200→ii. The blocks found from where the prior changes:\n   201→```python\n   202→block = (trials.probabilityLeft != trials.probabilityLeft.shift()).cumsum()\n   203→```\n   204→\n   205→iii. The trials table carries no block identifier, so the blocks have to be recovered from the prior.\n   206→\n   207→## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *trial_number_in_block*?\n   208→\n   209→i. The value is the position of a trial within its block, counted from zero.\n   210→\n   211→ii. The count within each block, and the broadcast:\n   212→```python\n   213→block = (trials.probabilityLeft != trials.probabilityLeft.shift()).cumsum()\n   214→```\n   215→\n   216→iii. The count is taken before the reaction time and no response filters are applied, so a trial that is later dropped still advances it and the number is the animal's real position in the block.\n   217→\n   218→## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *choice* derived from?\n   219→\n   220→i. One column of the trials table, `choice`, which is +1, -1 or 0. It is recoded to 0 for left and 1 for right, and the 0 entries, meaning the animal did not respond, are dropped with the trial.\n   221→\n   222→ii. The mapping, and where it is applied:\n   223→```python\n   224→CHOICE = {1.0: 0, -1.0: 1}       # +1 is a leftward choice, -1 rightward; 0 is a no response, dropped\n   225→```\n   226→\n   227→```python\n   228→'choice': trials.choice.map(CHOICE),\n   229→```\n   230→\n   231→iii. The IBL uses +1 for left choice and -1 for rightward choice. No response trials are dropped.\n   232→\n   233→## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *choice*?\n   234→\n   235→i. None beyond the recoding of +1 and -1 to 0 and 1 described above.\n   236→\n   237→ii. N/A\n   238→\n   239→iii. N/A\n   240→\n   241→## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *prior_probability_left* derived from?\n   242→\n   243→i. One column of the trials table, `probabilityLeft`, which takes the three values 0.2, 0.5 and 0.8 and is recoded to 0, 1 and 2.\n   244→\n   245→ii. The mapping, and where it is applied:\n   246→```python\n   247→PRIOR = {0.2: 0, 0.5: 1, 0.8: 2}\n   248→```\n   249→\n   250→```python\n   251→'prior': trials.probabilityLeft.map(PRIOR),\n   252→```\n   253→\n   254→iii. The three values are the block prior the task holds constant within a block, and the instructions give this mapping.\n   255→\n   256→## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *prior_probability_left*?\n   257→\n   258→i. None beyond the recoding of 0.2, 0.5 and 0.8 to 0, 1 and 2 described above.\n   259→\n   260→ii. N/A\n   261→\n   262→iii. N/A\n   263→\n   264→## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *wheel_speed* derived from?\n   265→\n   266→i. The wheel position and its timestamps, `_ibl_wheel.position` and `_ibl_wheel.timestamps`. `SessionLoader` turns them into a velocity, and the speed is the absolute value of that velocity.\n   267→\n   268→ii. The wheel, and the speed taken from it:\n   269→```python\n   270→loader.load_wheel()\n   271→```\n   272→\n   273→```python\n   274→speed = trial_traces(wheel.times, np.abs(wheel.velocity), stim_on)\n   275→```\n   276→\n   277→iii. The reference derives `'wheel-speed'` the same way, as `np.abs` of the velocity that `SessionLoader` returns.\n   278→\n   279→## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *wheel_speed*?\n   280→\n   281→i. Three steps. The wheel is recorded only when it moves, so `SessionLoader` first interpolates the position onto an even 1000 Hz grid and differentiates it into a velocity, applying a 20 Hz Butterworth low pass while doing so; the speed is the absolute value of that velocity, in radians per second. That trace is then resampled onto the 100 bin centres of each trial. Finally it is cut into three classes at the 33rd and 67th percentile of the session, so the three classes are equally sized within a session.\n   282→\n   283→ii. What `SessionLoader.load_wheel` does internally:\n   284→```python\n   285→self.wheel['position'], self.wheel['times'] = interpolate_position(\n   286→    wheel_raw['timestamps'], wheel_raw['position'], freq=fs)\n   287→self.wheel['velocity'], self.wheel['acceleration'] = velocity_filtered(\n   288→    self.wheel['position'], fs=fs, corner_frequency=corner_frequency, order=order)\n   289→```\n   290→\n   291→Resampling onto the bin centres, then splitting into three:\n   292→```python\n   293→traces.append(np.interp(onset + TIME, times[first:last], values[first:last]))\n   294→```\n   295→\n   296→```python\n   297→SPLIT = [100 / 3, 2 * 100 / 3]       # equal sized classes, from percentiles of each session's own trace\n   298→\n   299→def discretize(trace):\n   300→    \"\"\"Equal sized classes, split at percentiles of this session's own trace.\"\"\"\n   301→    return np.digitize(trace, np.percentile(trace, SPLIT))\n   302→```\n   303→\n   304→iii. The interpolation and the filter are not choices, they are what the IBL documentation recommends for computing wheel velocity and what `SessionLoader` does by default, so the reference and this conversion get the same trace.\n   305→\n   306→## 7-d. How is `output` *wheel_speed* aligned with the neural data?\n   307→\n   308→i. The wheel trace is sampled at the same 100 bin centres as the neural data, measured from the same stimulus onset, so the two share a time axis bin for bin.\n   309→\n   310→ii. The bin centres, and the wheel evaluated at them:\n   311→```python\n   312→TIME = EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                              # bin centres, the first decoder input\n   313→```\n   314→\n   315→```python\n   316→traces.append(np.interp(onset + TIME, times[first:last], values[first:last]))\n   317→```\n   318→\n   319→iii. The wheel is on the same session clock as the spikes, so evaluating it at the bin centres is all the alignment needed.\n   320→\n   321→## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *whisker_motion_energy* derived from?\n   322→\n   323→i. The motion energy of a side camera, `<side>Camera.ROIMotionEnergy` with its frame times `_ibl_<side>Camera.times`. It is a single value per video frame, computed by IBL over a square covering the whisker pad, and is used as released. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. Everything is read through the ONE api against the IBL cache in `data/one_cache`, never by opening files directly. The index of the brain-wide map release is loaded first, listing the sessions and the datasets each one holds. Searching that index returns an `eid`, the session identifier, and that one string is the only handle needed afterwards: every loader takes the `eid` and resolves the files itself. `SessionLoader` reads the trials table, the wheel and the camera motion energy of a session, and `SpikeSortingLoader` reads the spikes and clusters of one probe insertion, so it is called once per probe.\n\nii. The index, and the search that returns the session identifiers:\n```python\none.load_cache(tag=RELEASE)               # downloads the release index if it is not cached\neids, info = one.search(query_type='local', details=True)\n```\n\nEverything else follows from an `eid`:\n```python\nloader = SessionLoader(one=one, eid=eid)\nloader = SpikeSortingLoader(pid=pid, one=one, eid=eid, pname=name)\n```\n\niii. The API resolves a session and a dataset name to a path. Use the one.search to idenity unique session id to then locate the data files.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n\ni. The search returns the subject name alongside each `eid`, so no parsing of paths or file names is involved. Sessions are collected into a dictionary keyed by subject, which is the form the conversion iterates over. At assembly the subjects are the sorted unique names and `subject_idx` records, for each session, its index into that list. The 444 converted sessions come from 136 subjects, a median of three sessions per animal and up to thirteen.\n\nii. Grouping the sessions as they are selected:\n```python\nselected.setdefault(session['subject'], []).append(str(eid))\n```\n\nAt assembly:\n```python\nsubjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\nsub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n```\n\niii. The API already returns a unique subject id, so nothing has to be derived.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. A session is already the unit the release is organised by, and the search returns one `eid` per session, so nothing has to be split.\n\nii. The search returns the session identifiers:\n```python\neids, info = one.search(query_type='local', details=True)\n```\n\niii. No decision to make, the API returns sessions individually.\n\n## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n\ni. The trials table has one row per trial, so the split is given by the data.\n\nii. The trials table of a session:\n```python\nloader.load_trials()\n```\n\niii. No decision to make, the trials table is already one row per trial.\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Four things, all applied together. The reaction time, first wheel movement minus stimulus onset, has to fall between 80 ms and 2 s. Trials where the animal made no choice are dropped. Trials whose choice or block probability is missing are dropped. Finally the trial window has to be spanned by the wheel and the camera, which drops trials at the edges of a recording.\n\nii.\n```python\nkeep = (reaction >= MIN_RT) & (reaction <= MAX_RT)          # NaN compares false, so those go too\nkeep &= trials.choice.isin(CHOICE).to_numpy()               # drops the no response trials\nkeep &= trials.probabilityLeft.isin(PRIOR).to_numpy()\n\nreturn keep & covered(wheel_times, stim_on) & covered(camera_times, stim_on)\n```\n\niii. The reaction time bounds and the no-response exclusion are the reference paper's own trial mask, `min_rt=0.08, max_rt=2` with `exclude_nochoice=True`, and the data paper truncates its reaction time distribution at the same 80 ms and 2 s.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Two arrays per probe, `spikes.times` and `spikes.clusters`, which are the time of every spike and the cluster it was assigned to. The cluster table supplies the quality label and the anatomical location, but the neural array itself is built only from those two.\n\nii. Both come from the spike sorting loader:\n```python\nspikes, clusters, channels = loader.load_spike_sorting(check_hash=False)\n```\n\n```python\nunits.append(new_index[spikes['clusters'][keep]] + len(regions))\ntimes.append(spikes['times'][keep])\n```\n\niii. Spike time is directly avialible through the loader.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. Spikes are counted into 20 ms bins over the trial window, giving one count per unit per bin, and the counts are divided by the bin width to become a firing rate in Hz. When a session has two probes their units are pooled into one population, numbered continuously so that the second probe's units continue after the first probe's.\n\nii. Counting a trial, then converting to a rate:\n```python\nbin_index = np.floor((spike_time - T_START) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\nflat_index = spike_unit * N_BINS + bin_index\ncounts[trial] = np.bincount(flat_index, minlength=n_units * N_BINS).reshape(n_units, N_BINS)\n```\n\n```python\nrate = spike_counts(spike_times, spike_units, regions.size, stim_on) / BIN\n```\n\niii. The reference bins spike counts and does not smooth, so no smoothing is applied here either. Merging the probes of a session follows the reference's `merge_probes`, whose reasoning is that two probes in one session see the same behaviour and so are not independent recordings.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Only clusters whose `label` is 1 are kept. The label is a score of 0, 1/3, 2/3 or 1 that the IBL pipeline assigns from a set of spike sorting metrics, so requiring 1 keeps the units that pass all of them. Across the release this keeps 75,708 of 621,733 clusters, and in the converted data 73,044 units over 444 sessions, a median of 142 per session.\n\nii. The filter, applied per probe before the spikes are kept:\n```python\n# spikes.clusters holds the row of the cluster table, so a per cluster flag\n# can be indexed by it directly to say whether each spike survives\nis_good = clusters['label'] >= QC_LABEL\nkeep = is_good[spikes['clusters']]\n```\n\niii. This is the curation of the data paper, which reports 75,708 well-isolated neurons averaging 108 per probe; applying `label >= 1` reproduce their number.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Every stream of a session is already expressed in seconds on one shared clock: spike times, trial event times, wheel timestamps and camera frame times all run from the start of the recording, because IBL synchronises them upstream through a sync channel common to the ephys and the behaviour rig. Aligning a trial is therefore just subtracting its stimulus onset from the spike times, which puts zero at the event and leaves the window running from -0.5 s to 1.5 s around it.\n\nii. Subtracting the onset:\n```python\nspike_time = times[begin[trial]:end[trial]] - onset      # time from stimulus onset\n```\n\niii. Because the clocks are already aligned there is additional alignment required.\n\n## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n\ni. The 2 s window is divided into 100 bins of 20 ms; no resample or interpolation is applied.\n\nii. The bin grid, and the bin each spike falls in:\n```python\nBIN = 0.02\nN_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))            # 100\n```\n\n```python\nbin_index = np.floor((spike_time - T_START) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. The reference code sets `'binsize': 0.02`, and both papers use 20 ms bins; the method paper describes exactly this configuration, \"split into 2-s trials, each divided into 20-ms bins, producing T = 100 time steps\".\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *time_from_stimulus_onset* derived from?\n\ni. From `stimOn_times` in the trials table, which is the event every trial is aligned on. The window is -0.5 to 1.5 s around that onset in 20 ms bins, and the input is the centre of each of the 100 bins, so one set of values serves every trial.\n\nii. The onset each trial is aligned on, and the window taken around it:\n```python\nstim_on = trials.stimOn_times.to_numpy()[keep]\n```\n```python\nT_START, T_STOP = -0.5, 1.5      # decoding window around stimulus onset, following the reference code\nBIN = 0.02\nN_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))            # 100\nEDGES = T_START + BIN * np.arange(N_BINS + 1)\nTIME = EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                              # bin centres, the first decoder input\n```\n\niii. The window and the bin size are the decoding parameters of the reference code.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *time_from_stimulus_onset*?\n\ni. There is no processing, the variable is defined by us.\n\nii. N/A\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *trial_number_in_block* derived from?\n\ni. From `probabilityLeft` in the trials table, which is held constant within a block, so a change of its value starts a new one.\n\nii. The blocks found from where the prior changes:\n```python\nblock = (trials.probabilityLeft != trials.probabilityLeft.shift()).cumsum()\n```\n\niii. The value is the position of a trial within its block, counted from zero.\n\nii. The count within each block, and the broadcast:\n```python\nblock = (trials.probabilityLeft != trials.probabilityLeft.shift()).cumsum()\n```\n\niii. The count is taken before the reaction time and no response filters are applied, so a trial that is later dropped still advances it and the number is the animal's real position in the block.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *choice* derived from?\n\ni. One column of the trials table, `choice`, which is +1, -1 or 0. It is recoded to 0 for left and 1 for right, and the 0 entries, meaning the animal did not respond, are dropped with the trial.\n\nii. The mapping, and where it is applied:\n```python\nCHOICE = {1.0: 0, -1.0: 1}       # +1 is a leftward choice, -1 rightward; 0 is a no response, dropped\n```\n\n```python\n'choice': trials.choice.map(CHOICE),\n```\n\niii. The IBL uses +1 for left choice and -1 for rightward choice. No response trials are dropped.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *choice*?\n\ni. What variables in the raw data is `output` *wheel_speed* derived from?\n\ni. The wheel position and its timestamps, `_ibl_wheel.position` and `_ibl_wheel.timestamps`. `SessionLoader` turns them into a velocity, and the speed is the absolute value of that velocity.\n\nii. The reference derives `'wheel-speed'` the same way, as `np.abs` of the velocity that `SessionLoader` returns.\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *wheel_speed*?\n\ni. Three steps. The wheel is recorded only when it moves, so `SessionLoader` first interpolates the position onto an even 1000 Hz grid and differentiates it into a velocity, applying a 20 Hz Butterworth low pass while doing so; the speed is the absolute value of that velocity, in radians per second. That trace is then resampled onto the 100 bin centres of each trial. Finally it is cut into three classes at the 33rd and 67th percentile of the session, so the three classes are equally sized within a session.\n\nii. What `SessionLoader.load_wheel` does internally:\n```python\nself.wheel['position'], self.wheel['times'] = interpolate_position(\n    wheel_raw['timestamps'], wheel_raw['position'], freq=fs)\nself.wheel['velocity'], self.wheel['acceleration'] = velocity_filtered(\n    self.wheel['position'], fs=fs, corner_frequency=corner_frequency, order=order)\n```\n\nResampling onto the bin centres, then splitting into three:\n```python\ntraces.append(np.interp(onset + TIME, times[first:last], values[first:last]))\n```\n\n```python\nSPLIT = [100 / 3, 2 * 100 / 3]       # equal sized classes, from percentiles of each session's own trace\n\ndef discretize(trace):\n    \"\"\"Equal sized classes, split at percentiles of this session's own trace.\"\"\"\n    return np.digitize(trace, np.percentile(trace, SPLIT))\n```\n\niii. The interpolation and the filter are not choices, they are what the IBL documentation recommends for computing wheel velocity and what `SessionLoader` does by default, so the reference and this conversion get the same trace.\n\n## 7-d. The wheel is on the same session clock as the spikes, so evaluating it at the bin centres is all the alignment needed.\n\n## 8-a. The motion energy of a side camera, `<side>Camera.ROIMotionEnergy` with its frame times `_ibl_<side>Camera.times`. Missing data is dropped: A trial whose window is not spanned by the wheel or the camera is dropped; A session left with no unit or fewer than two trials is dropped.\n\nii. decoding window around stimulus onset, following the reference code\nBIN = 0.02\nN_BINS = int(round((T_STOP - T_START) / BIN))            # 100\nEDGES = T_START + BIN * np.arange(N_BINS + 1)\nTIME = EDGES[:-1] + BIN / 2                              # bin centres, the first decoder input\n\n# reaction time bounds of the paper, which truncates the distribution at 80 ms and 2 s;\nMIN_RT, MAX_RT = 0.08, 2.0\n\nQC_LABEL = 1.0                   # the \"stringent quality control\" of the paper, 75,708 units of 621,733\nSPLIT = [100 / 3, 2 * 100 / 3]       # equal sized classes, from percentiles of each session's own trace\n\nCHOICE = {1.0: 0, -1.0: 1}       # +1 is a leftward choice, -1 rightward; 0 is a no response, dropped\nPRIOR = {0.2: 0, 0.5: 1, 0.8: 2}\nMOVEMENT = ['low', 'medium', 'high']\n\nINPUT_NAMES = ['time_from_stimulus_onset', 'trial_number_in_block']\nOUTPUT_NAMES = ['choice', 'prior_probability_left', 'wheel_speed', 'whisker_motion_energy']\nOUTPUT_VALUES = [['left', 'right'], ['0.2', '0.5', '0.8'], MOVEMENT, MOVEMENT]\n\n# the cache location is not known until --datadir is parsed, and the connection cannot be\n# shared across a fork anyway, so connect() fills this in per process rather than at import\none = None\nbrain_regions = BrainRegions()\n\n\ndef connect(datadir):\n    \"\"\"Point the module-level ONE client at the cache under datadir.\n\n    datadir: directory holding the one_cache/ folder.\n    Sets the global `one`; returns nothing.\n    \"\"\"\n    global one\n    one = ONE(base_url='https://openalyx.internationalbrainlab.org', password='international',\n              silent=True, cache_dir=os.path.join(datadir, 'one_cache'))\n\n\n# --- sessions ---\n\ndef select_sessions():\n    \"\"\"Released sessions that have everything the decoder needs, grouped by subject.\"\"\"\n    one.load_cache(tag=RELEASE)               # downloads the release index if it is not cached\n\n    required = ['spikes.times.npy', 'spikes.clusters.npy',          # ephys\n                '_ibl_trials.table.pqt',                            # task\n                '_ibl_wheel.position.npy', '_ibl_wheel.timestamps.npy']\n\n    keep = set(one.search(query_type='local', datasets=required))\n    keep &= (set(one.search(query_type='local', datasets=['leftCamera.ROIMotionEnergy.npy',\n                                                          '_ibl_leftCamera.times.npy']))\n             | set(one.search(query_type='local', datasets=['rightCamera.ROIMotionEnergy.npy',\n                                                            '_ibl_rightCamera.times.npy'])))\n\n    eids, info = one.search(query_type='local', details=True)\n    selected = {}\n    for eid, session in zip(eids, info):\n        if eid in keep:\n            selected.setdefault(session['subject'], []).append(str(eid))\n    return {subject: selected[subject] for subject in sorted(selected)}\n\n\ndef camera_view(eid):\n    \"\"\"The side camera this session has whisker motion energy for, left preferred.\"\"\"\n    for view in ('left', 'right'):\n        if len(one.list_datasets(eid, filename=f'{view}Camera.ROIMotionEnergy.npy',\n                                 query_type='local')):\n            return view\n    raise ValueError(f'no whisker motion energy for {eid}')\n\n\n# --- trials ---\n\ndef trial_variables(trials):\n    \"\"\"Choice, block prior and trial number in block, one row per trial.\"\"\"\n    # probabilityLeft is constant within a block, so a change of value starts a new block\n    block = (trials.probabilityLeft != trials.probabilityLeft.shift()).cumsum()\n\n    return pd.DataFrame({'choice': trials.choice.map(CHOICE),\n                         'prior': trials.probabilityLeft.map(PRIOR),\n                         'trial_in_block': trials.groupby(block).cumcount()})\n\n\ndef covered(times, stim_on):\n    \"\"\"Trials whose window is spanned by this stream, allowing the one bin gap the reference allows.\"\"\"\n    start, stop = stim_on + T_START, stim_on + T_STOP\n    begin = np.searchsorted(times, start, side='right')      # first sample inside the window\n    end = np.searchsorted(times, stop, side='left') - 1       # last sample inside the window\n\n    # a trial with no sample at all, or a gap at either edge, has no usable trace\n    inside = begin <= end\n    first = np.where(inside, times[np.clip(begin, 0, times.size - 1)], np.inf)\n    last = np.where(inside, times[np.clip(end, 0, times.size - 1)], -np.inf)\n    return inside & (np.abs(start - first) <= BIN) & (np.abs(stop - last) <= BIN)\n\n\ndef usable_trials(trials, wheel_times, camera_times):\n    \"\"\"Trials the paper would keep, that also have a full wheel and camera window.\"\"\"\n    stim_on = trials.stimOn_times.to_numpy()\n    reaction = trials.firstMovement_times.to_numpy() - stim_on\n\n    keep = (reaction >= MIN_RT) & (reaction <= MAX_RT)          # NaN compares false, so those go too\n    keep &= trials.choice.isin(CHOICE).to_numpy()               # drops the no response trials\n    keep &= trials.probabilityLeft.isin(PRIOR).to_numpy()\n\n    return keep & covered(wheel_times, stim_on) & covered(camera_times, stim_on)\n\n\n# --- behavior ---\n\ndef window_slice(times, stim_on):\n    \"\"\"Index range of each trial window, so a stream is sliced rather than rescanned per trial.\"\"\"\n    window_start = stim_on + T_START\n    window_stop = stim_on + T_STOP\n\n    # the samples are in time order, so each window is one contiguous range of them\n    begin = np.searchsorted(times, window_start)\n    end = np.searchsorted(times, window_stop)\n\n    return begin, end\n\n\ndef trial_traces(times, values, stim_on):\n    \"\"\"A continuous trace resampled onto the same time grid for every trial.\"\"\"\n    begin, end = window_slice(times, stim_on)\n\n    traces = []\n    for trial, onset in enumerate(stim_on):\n        # one sample either side of the window, so the first and last bin centre are\n        # interpolated rather than clamped to the nearest sample inside it\n        first = max(begin[trial] - 1, 0)\n        last = end[trial] + 1\n\n        traces.append(np.interp(onset + TIME, times[first:last], values[first:last]))\n\n    return np.array(traces)\n\n\ndef discretize(trace):\n    \"\"\"Equal sized classes, split at percentiles of this session's own trace.\"\"\"\n    return np.digitize(trace, np.percentile(trace, SPLIT))\n\n\n# --- neural ---\n\ndef load_neural(eid):\n    \"\"\"Spike times and good clusters of a session, merged over its probes.\"\"\"\n    times, units, regions = [], [], []\n    for pid, name in zip(*one.eid2pid(eid)):\n        loader = SpikeSortingLoader(pid=pid, one=one, eid=eid, pname=name)\n\n        # skip the md5 check, which rereads every spike file and draws its own progress bar\n        spikes, clusters, channels = loader.load_spike_sorting(check_hash=False)\n\n        # a session can hold an insertion whose spike sorting was never released\n        if not clusters:\n            continue\n\n        clusters = loader.merge_clusters(spikes, clusters, channels)   # attaches metrics and histology\n\n        # spikes.clusters holds the row of the cluster table, so a per cluster flag\n        # can be indexed by it directly to say whether each spike survives\n        is_good = clusters['label'] >= QC_LABEL\n        keep = is_good[spikes['clusters']]\n\n        # renumber the surviving clusters, continuing the count from the previous probe\n        new_index = np.cumsum(is_good) - 1\n        units.append(new_index[spikes['clusters'][keep]] + len(regions))\n        times.append(spikes['times'][keep])\n\n        # Beryl is the coarser atlas the reference code uses, e.g. DG-sg and DG-mo both become DG\n        regions.extend(brain_regions.acronym2acronym(clusters['acronym'][is_good], mapping='Beryl'))\n\n    times, units = np.concatenate(times), np.concatenate(units)\n\n    # sort the merged probes by spike time, as the reference does, so trials can be sliced\n    order = np.argsort(times, kind='stable')\n    return times[order], units[order], np.array(regions)\n\n\ndef spike_counts(times, units, n_units, stim_on):\n    \"\"\"Spike count per unit and bin, for every trial.\"\"\"\n    counts = np.empty((stim_on.size, n_units, N_BINS))\n    begin, end = window_slice(times, stim_on)\n\n    for trial, onset in enumerate(stim_on):\n        spike_time = times[begin[trial]:end[trial]] - onset      # time from stimulus onset\n        spike_unit = units[begin[trial]:end[trial]]\n\n        bin_index = np.floor((spike_time - T_START) / BIN).astype(np.int64)\n        \n        # a spike sitting on the window edge can round out of range once the onset is subtracted\n        bin_index = np.clip(bin_index, 0, N_BINS - 1)\n\n        # one flat index per spike lets a single bincount fill the whole unit by bin grid\n        flat_index = spike_unit * N_BINS + bin_index\n        counts[trial] = np.bincount(flat_index, minlength=n_units * N_BINS).reshape(n_units, N_BINS)\n\n    return counts\n\n\n# --- session ---\n\ndef process_session(eid, subject):\n    \"\"\"One session as lists of per trial neural, input and output arrays.\"\"\"\n    loader = SessionLoader(one=one, eid=eid)\n    loader.load_trials()\n    loader.load_wheel()\n    view = camera_view(eid)\n    loader.load_motion_energy(views=[view])\n\n    trials, wheel = loader.trials, loader.wheel\n    motion_energy = loader.motion_energy[f'{view}Camera']\n\n    variables = trial_variables(trials)                 # before filtering, so the block count is real\n    keep = usable_trials(trials, wheel.times.to_numpy(), motion_energy.times.to_numpy())\n    variables = variables[keep]\n    stim_on = trials.stimOn_times.to_numpy()[keep]\n\n    # no trial survived the mask, so there is nothing to load or bin\n    if stim_on.size == 0:\n        return {'session_id': str(eid), 'subject': subject, 'camera': view,\n                'neural': [], 'input': [], 'output': [],\n                'n_trials': 0, 'n_units': 0, 'regions': np.array([])}\n\n    speed = trial_traces(wheel.times, np.abs(wheel.velocity), stim_on)\n    whisker = trial_traces(motion_energy.times, motion_energy.iloc[:, 1], stim_on)\n\n    spike_times, spike_units, regions = load_neural(eid)\n    rate = spike_counts(spike_times, spike_units, regions.size, stim_on) / BIN\n\n    # the split is over the whole session, so both traces are discretized once, not per trial\n    speed_class = discretize(speed)\n    whisker_class = discretize(whisker)\n\n    neural = [trial.astype(np.float32) for trial in rate]\n    inputs, outputs = [], []\n    for trial in range(stim_on.size):\n        row = variables.iloc[trial]\n        inputs.append(np.stack([TIME, np.full(N_BINS, row.trial_in_block)]).astype(np.float32))\n        outputs.append(np.stack([np.full(N_BINS, row.choice), np.full(N_BINS, row.prior),\n                                 speed_class[trial], whisker_class[trial]]).astype(np.int8))\n\n    return {'session_id': str(eid), 'subject': subject, 'camera': view,\n            'neural': neural, 'input': inputs, 'output': outputs,\n            'n_trials': len(neural), 'n_units': regions.size, 'regions': regions}\n\n\ndef assemble(results):\n    \"\"\"Stitch per-session results into the target dictionary.\"\"\"\n    subjects = sorted({r['subject'] for r in results})\n    sub_ix = {s: i for i, s in enumerate(subjects)}\n\n    regions = sorted({region for r in results for region in r['regions']})\n    reg_ix = {region: i for i, region in enumerate(regions)}\n\n    return {\n        'neural': [r['neural'] for r in results],\n        'input': [r['input'] for r in results],\n        'output': [r['output'] for r in results],\n\n        'subjects': subjects,\n        'subject_idx': np.array([sub_ix[r['subject']] for r in results], np.int32),\n\n        'brain_regions': regions,\n        'brain_region_idx': [np.array([reg_ix[x] for x in r['regions']], np.int32) for r in results],\n\n        'input_names': INPUT_NAMES,\n        'output_names': OUTPUT_NAMES,\n        'output_values': OUTPUT_VALUES,\n\n        'metadata': {\n            'task_description':\n                'Head-fixed mice turn a wheel to report the side of a visual grating. The '\n                'probability that the stimulus appears on the left is held constant within a '\n                'block and switches between 0.2 and 0.8.',\n            'time_bin_size': BIN * 1000.0,          # ms\n            'temporal_alignment_event': 'visual stimulus onset (stimOn_times)',\n            'off_start': T_START,\n            'off_end': T_STOP,\n\n            'dataset': 'IBL brain-wide map, release tag %s' % RELEASE,\n            'neural_units': 'Hz',\n            'session_info': [\n                {'session_id': r['session_id'], 'subject': r['subject'], 'camera': r['camera'],\n                 'n_trials': r['n_trials'], 'n_units': r['n_units']}\n                for r in results\n            ],\n        },\n    }\n\n\ndef worker_setup(datadir):\n    \"\"\"Give each process its own database connection, which cannot be shared across a fork.\n\n    datadir: directory holding the one_cache/ folder, forwarded from --datadir.\n    \"\"\"\n    connect(datadir)\n    one.load_cache(tag=RELEASE)\n\n\ndef main():\n    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=__doc__)\n    ap.add_argument('outfile', help='output pickle path')\n    ap.add_argument('--full', action='store_true', help='process all sessions (default)')\n    ap.add_argument('--sample', action='store_true', help='process only 2 sessions')\n    ap.add_argument('--workers', type=int, default=10, help='sessions to process in parallel')\n    ap.add_argument('--datadir', default=DATA_DIR,\n                    help='directory holding the one_cache/ folder (default: %(default)s)')\n    args = ap.parse_args()\n\n    connect(args.datadir)\n    selected = select_sessions()\n    pairs = [(eid, subject) for subject, eids in selected.items() for eid in eids]\n    if args.sample:\n        pairs = pairs[:2]\n    print('%d sessions, %d subjects, %d workers'\n          % (len(pairs), len({s for _, s in pairs}), args.workers), flush=True)\n\n    t0 = time.time()\n\n    # sessions are independent, so they are processed out of order and put back by index\n    ordered = [None] * len(pairs)\n    with ProcessPoolExecutor(max_workers=args.workers, initializer=worker_setup,\n                             initargs=(args.datadir,)) as pool:\n        futures = {pool.submit(process_session, eid, subject): index\n                   for index, (eid, subject) in enumerate(pairs)}\n\n        progress = tqdm(as_completed(futures), total=len(futures), unit='session')\n        for future in progress:\n            eid, subject = pairs[futures[future]]\n            try:\n                res = future.result()\n            except Exception as error:\n                print('failed %s: %s: %s' % (eid[:8], type(error).__name__, error), flush=True)\n                continue\n\n            # a session with no surviving unit or trial will be skipped\n            if res['n_units'] == 0 or res['n_trials'] < 2:\n                print('skipped %s: %d trials, %d units'\n                      % (res['session_id'][:8], res['n_trials'], res['n_units']), flush=True)\n                continue\n\n            ordered[futures[future]] = res\n            progress.set_postfix_str('%s %s %d trials %d units'\n                                     % (res['session_id'][:8], subject, res['n_trials'], res['n_units']))\n\n    results = [res for res in ordered if res is not None]\n    data = assemble(results)\n    print('\\nconverted in %.1f s' % (time.time() - t0), flush=True)\n    print('sessions %d | subjects %d | regions %d | trials %d | units %d'\n          % (len(results), len(data['subjects']), len(data['brain_regions']),\n             sum(r['n_trials'] for r in results), sum(r['n_units'] for r in results)), flush=True)\n\n    with open(args.outfile, 'wb') as f:\n        pickle.dump(data, f, protocol=pickle.HIGHEST_PROTOCOL)\n    print('wrote %s (%.1f GB)' % (args.outfile, os.path.getsize(args.outfile) / 1e9), flush=True)\n\n\nif __name__ == '__main__':\n    main()\n","numLines":401,"startLine":1,"totalLines":401}}}

Check revision directories (format: #YYYY-MM-DD#)\n    60→    candidates = []\n    61→    if base_path.exists():\n    62→        for item in base_path.iterdir():\n    63→            if item.is_dir() and item.name.startswith('#') and item.name.endswith('#'):\n    64→                for f in item.iterdir():\n    65→                    if filename_pattern in f.name:\n    66→                        candidates.append(f)\n    67→        # Also check base path directly\n    68→        for f in base_path.iterdir():\n    69→            if not f.is_dir() and filename_pattern in f.name:\n    70→                candidates.append(f)\n    71→\n    72→    if not candidates:\n    73→        return None\n    74→    # Sort by revision date (newest first), files without revision come last\n    75→    def sort_key(p):\n    76→        parts = p.parts\n    77→        for part in parts:\n    78→            if part.startswith('#') and part.endswith('#'):\n    79→                return part\n    80→        return '#0000-00-00#'\n    81→    candidates.sort(key=sort_key, reverse=True)\n    82→    return candidates[0]\n    83→\n    84→\n    85→def load_trials(alf_path):\n    86→    \"\"\"Load trials table from session.\"\"\"\n    87→    trials_file = find_latest_revision(alf_path, '_ibl_trials.table.pqt')\n    88→    if trials_file is None:\n    89→        raise FileNotFoundError(f\"No trials table found in {alf_path}\")\n    90→    trials = pd.read_parquet(trials_file)\n    91→    return trials\n    92→\n    93→\n    94→def create_trial_mask(trials):\n    95→    \"\"\"Create boolean mask for trial inclusion following reference code.\"\"\"\n    96→    mask = pd.Series(True, index=trials.index)\n    97→\n    98→    #
